# ROUND 2/10

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time: score increases for every **stationary** robot → robots should **move**
- After switch time: score increases for every **moving** robot → robots should **stop**
- Switch happens between steps 400-600; at switch time, one wall lights up GREEN
- Robots need to: move before switch, then stop after detecting green

**Key insight:** The optimal strategy is:
1. Move (explore) before the switch
2. When green is detected (C=7, color 2=green), stop

The champion is a 1-state exploration FSM scoring 16104. This means pure movement is decent but we can do better by stopping when green is detected.

**Design strategy:**
- State 0: Exploration (moving) → transition to Stop when green perceived
- State 1: Stop → maybe transition back if green disappears (but green stays lit)

The best performer (16104) is pure exploration. We need to add a green-detection stop mechanism. Let me think about what scores better:
- Running time ~1200 steps, switch at ~500, so ~700 steps moving + 500 steps stopped would be ideal
- Pure exploration: before switch ~500 steps × 28 robots stationary moments + after switch ~700 steps × 28 robots moving
- Actually with pure exploration: before switch robots move (good), after switch robots move (bad, ~700×28 = 19600 penalty)
- With stop on green: before switch robots move (good), after switch robots stop (good)

So the ideal is: move until green detected, then stop forever. This should score much lower.

**Machines to generate:**
1. Champion copy (exact)
2-5. Exploitation of champion (vary rwm)
6-9. 2-state: Explore → Stop on green detection
10-11. 2-state with additional transitions
12-14. 3-state variations

For green detection: color 2 = green (based on the color list: 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan). The wall lights up GREEN, so C=7 with l=2.

## Reasoning

**Analysis of results:**
- Champion: GoToColor(green) → Stop on green detection. Score 11296.98
- Key insight confirmed: Move toward green wall (GoToColor clr=2), then when green perceived, stop permanently
- Pure exploration FSMs scored ~16000 (worse)
- The champion uses GoToColor(green) as initial state, which moves robots toward the green wall efficiently

**Why GoToColor(green) beats Exploration:**
- GoToColor actively seeks the green wall, so robots reach it and stop sooner
- After the switch, robots stop as soon as they perceive green (which they're already near)
- Before switch, robots are moving (good), heading toward green wall

**Exploitation strategy:**
1. Keep champion exact
2. Vary p0x0 (probability of transitioning to stop on green detection): try 0.7, 0.8, 0.95, 1.0
3. Try different velocity/approach: maybe adding a second transition from state 0
4. Try GoToColor with additional neighbor-based transitions
5. Explore 3-state machines: Explore → GoToColor(green) → Stop

**Key corrections:**
- LED colors must be {0,4,5,6} only - avoid 1,2,3

**New ideas:**
- State 0: GoToColor(green), transition to Stop when see green (high prob)
- Adding a fallback: if no green seen, keep moving; once green detected, stop
- Try GoAwayColor from non-green to keep moving before switch
- 3-state: Explore → GoToColor(green) → Stop

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis of results:**
- Champion: GoToColor(green) + random-stop fallback → Stop on green detection. Score 9112
- The key structure: State 0 = GoToColor(green clr=2), transitions: (1) on perceive green → Stop, (2) random small prob → Stop
- State 1 = Stop, tiny random transition (0.001) back to... wait, it transitions to state 0 (itself only other state) - but p=0.001 is very small so rarely goes back

**Understanding the champion:**
- `--n0x1 0 --c0x1 5 --p0x1 0.002`: random transition with prob 0.002 to state 1 (Stop)
- `--n1x0 0 --c1x0 5 --p1x0 0.001`: random transition from Stop back to GoToColor with prob 0.001 (very rare)
- So robots move toward green, when they see green they stop (prob 0.9), occasionally stop randomly, and almost never restart

**Exploitation ideas:**
1. Vary p0x0 (green detection prob): 0.85, 0.9, 0.95, 1.0
2. Vary p0x1 (random stop prob): 0.001, 0.002, 0.003, 0.005
3. Vary p1x0 (restart prob): 0.0001, 0.0005, 0.001, 0.002
4. Try 3-state variations
5. Try making Stop truly absorbing (no outgoing transition... but rules say n≥1)

**Key observation:** The small random-stop prob (0.002) in state 0 helps. Before the switch, robots are moving (GoToColor counts as moving). The random stop is bad before switch but tiny. After switch, green detection triggers stop quickly.

The champion has a very low restart probability (0.001) from Stop - robots essentially stay stopped once they stop.

Let me focus on fine-tuning the champion's parameters and a few explorations.

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Analysis:**
- Champion: GoToColor(green) with two transitions: (1) perceive green → Stop (p=0.9), (2) random → Stop (p=0.002). Stop state has tiny restart prob (0.0001).
- Key finding: Very small restart probability (0.0001) is better than larger values
- The random-stop fallback (p=0.002) helps some robots stop early by chance
- Lower restart probability from Stop is clearly better

**What's working:**
- State 0: GoToColor(green), transitions to Stop on green detection OR tiny random chance
- State 1: Stop, very tiny random restart (0.0001 is best so far)

**Parameter space to explore:**
1. p0x0 (green detection): 0.85, 0.9, 0.95, 1.0 → champion uses 0.9
2. p0x1 (random stop): 0.001, 0.002, 0.003 → champion uses 0.002
3. p1x0 (restart from stop): 0.0001 is best, try even smaller: 0.00005, 0.00001, 0.0
4. Try making Stop truly have no restart effect by using p1x0=0.0 (but minimum might be 0.0001)

**New ideas to explore:**
- Can we make the restart probability effectively 0? p=0.0 might be allowed
- Try p1x0=0.0 (if allowed) to make Stop absorbing
- Add a second green-detection transition from Stop state (to reinforce stopping)
- 3-state: GoToColor → Stop → GoToColor (with very different structure)

**Key insight:** The random stop (p=0.002) from GoToColor state is actually helping before the switch time by occasionally stopping robots... wait no, before switch stationary robots are penalized. So random stops HURT before switch. But they help after switch for robots that haven't seen green yet.

Actually: after switch, robots that haven't seen green yet keep moving (bad). The random stop helps those robots. The green-detection stop is the main mechanism.

Let me try even lower p0x1 (random stop) to reduce pre-switch penalty, and keep p1x0 very low.

- State 2: GoToColor(green), → State 1 (Stop) on green detection (p=0.9), → State 1 on random (p=0.002)

Wait - states 0 and 2 are identical GoToColor(green) machines both pointing to State 1 (Stop). State 1 can restart to State 0. This is essentially: GoToColor → Stop (with tiny restart back to GoToColor). The 3rd state seems redundant but the structure differs slightly - when restarting from Stop, it goes back to State 0 (not State 2). State 2 is only reachable if... actually it's not reachable from State 1! State 1 only transitions to state 0 (D=0 means the first "other" state, which with 3 states from state 1 would be state 0). State 2 is never reached. So effectively it's the same as the 2-state machine but scores better due to randomness in evaluation.

**Key insight:** p1x0=0 (Score 7329) is also excellent - stopping is truly absorbing. The champion uses 0.0001.

**What to try:**
1. Champion exact copy
2. 3-state variant with p values tweaked
3. 2-state with p1x0=0, varying p0x0 and p0x1
4. Try 3-state where State 2 IS reachable and does something useful
5. Try making state 1 restart go to a different GoToColor state
6. Try 4-state machine

**Key parameters to vary:**
- p0x0 (green detect): 0.85, 0.9, 0.95, 1.0
- p0x1 (random stop): 0.001, 0.002, 0.003
- p1x0 (restart): 0.0, 0.00005, 0.0001

## Reasoning

**Analysis of champion (score 6410):**
- 3-state machine: States 0 and 2 are identical GoToColor(green), both transition to State 1 (Stop) on green detection (p=0.9) or random (p=0.003)
- State 1: Stop, tiny restart (p=0.0001)
- State 2 is unreachable (State 1 only restarts to State 0)
- The key improvement: p0x1=0.003 (random stop) beats p0x1=0.002

**Key findings:**
- p0x1=0.003 (random stop from GoToColor) is better than 0.002 or 0.001
- p1x0=0.0001 (restart from Stop) is good; p=0.0 also works well
- p0x0=0.9 (green detection probability) seems optimal
- 3-state and 4-state topologies with duplicate GoToColor states perform similarly to 2-state

**What to explore:**
1. Try p0x1=0.004, 0.005 (higher random stop probability)
2. Try p0x0=0.85 with p0x1=0.003
3. Try p1x0=0.0 with p0x1=0.003
4. Try p0x1=0.0025, 0.0035 (fine-tuning around 0.003)
5. Try 4-state with p0x1=0.003
6. Explore whether adding neighbor-count condition helps

The pattern is clear: the structure is locked in, now fine-tuning p0x1 around 0.003 and p1x0 around 0.0001.

## Reasoning

**Analysis of results:**
- Champion: p0x1=0.004 (random stop), score 6162.95
- Very close scores for p0x1 in range [0.003, 0.004, 0.0035]
- p0x0=0.9 (green detection) appears optimal
- p1x0=0.0001 is good, p1x0=0.0 also works well (6170)
- p0x1=0.006 is too high (7039), so the sweet spot is around 0.003-0.005

**Fine-tuning around champion:**
- Try p0x1 = 0.0038, 0.0042, 0.0045, 0.005
- Try p1x0 = 0.0, 0.00005, 0.0001, 0.0002
- The structure is essentially fixed: 3-state with states 0 and 2 being identical GoToColor(green) machines, state 1 being Stop

**Key observation:** States 0 and 2 are identical and state 2 is unreachable (state 1 only restarts to state 0). The benefit of 3-state over 2-state might just be noise. But since the champion IS 3-state, keep it.

**Exploration:** 
- Try 4-state with p0x1=0.004 (same pattern, extra redundant state)
- Try different p0x0 values: 0.88, 0.92

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 28 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.44 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
